# Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models

Haoyi Qiu Affiliation: Meta Superintelligence Labs
Affiliation: University of California, Los Angeles Work done during
internship at Meta    Akshat Agarwal Affiliation: Meta Superintelligence
Labs

###### Abstract

On-policy self-distillation (OPSD) trains a student on next-token
targets from a teacher, a frozen copy of the student that additionally
receives privileged information, such as a worked solution, hidden from
the student. Whether student gains reflect that information remains
unclear. Taking the worked solution as privileged information, we audit
four stages across three Qwen3 scales on mathematics: Source, whether
the teacher can use the solution; Encoding, whether the resulting token
targets are useful; Transmission, whether the student learns from the
targets; and Expression, whether the student applies the learned
behavior without the solution. With the solution, the teacher solves
hard problems and recovers from student errors, yet the teacher does not
reliably recognize errors, and its token targets do not favor correct
trajectories. Repeated training makes successes within the student’s
reach more frequent rather than unlocking new ones, and corrections the
student learns to predict are not reliably expressed in free generation.
To isolate the solution’s contribution, we vary its availability
separately from teacher and student thinking modes. The largest gains
occur when a teacher in thinking mode supervises a student trained and
evaluated without an explicit thinking trace, and problem-only teachers
often match or exceed privileged-solution teachers: the gain tracks the
teacher’s thinking mode rather than the solution, a pattern we call
reasoning arbitrage. Accuracy gains alone therefore do not establish
transfer of reasoning from privileged solutions: bottlenecks lie after
Source, across Encoding, Transmission, and Expression. Future
evaluations must measure what the solution adds to otherwise identical
training.

^(†)^(†)correspondence: Haoyi Qiu
([haoyiqiu@cs.ucla.edu](mailto:haoyiqiu@cs.ucla.edu))^(†)^(†)date:
October 15, 2026

## 1 Introduction

Privileged supervision offers a route to self-improving reasoning
models. On-policy self-distillation (OPSD) extends on-policy
distillation ([Agarwal et al., 2024](#bib.bib4)): after the student
generates a response, a frozen copy of the same model conditions on
privileged information, here a worked solution (the privileged
solution), and scores the rollout at each prefix, providing dense
next-token supervision ([Zhao et al., 2026a](#bib.bib5); [Shenfeld et
al., 2026](#bib.bib6); [Hübotter et al., 2026](#bib.bib7)). Unlike
outcome-based reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards ([Shao et
al., 2024](#bib.bib23); [Guo et al., 2025](#bib.bib24)), this objective
supplies a training signal at every response position. OPSD asks one
privileged model to act as _solver_, _evaluator_, and _teacher_. But
solving with the solution does not imply recognizing student errors, nor
does recognition imply producing useful targets.

Figure 1: Thinking-ON teachers improve Thinking-OFF generation at 4B and
8B with or without the privileged solution. (a) Avg@12 by teacher and
rollout modes, averaged over AIME 2024, AIME 2025, AIME 2026, HMMT Nov
2025, and HMMT Feb 2026. Rows give evaluation modes. (b)
Privileged-solution versus problem-only teachers with a Thinking-ON
teacher and Thinking-OFF rollouts. All teachers in (a) receive the
solution, and (b) varies only teacher access to it. Base is the starting
checkpoint in each evaluation mode. Trained curves average three
training seeds, while Base averages three decoding seeds. Labels show
Step-100 changes from Base (pp). Shading shows descriptive 95% bootstrap
intervals.

The privileged solution is not the teacher’s only advantage over the
student. The teacher’s thinking mode can also affect student gains, so
attributing those gains to the solution requires a control. Qwen3’s chat
template enables or disables a dedicated thinking block (Thinking ON or
OFF) ([Yang et al., 2025](#bib.bib13)), and we set this mode
independently for teacher scoring, student rollouts, and evaluation.
Across Qwen3-1.7B, 4B, and 8B and five competition-math benchmarks (AIME
2024, 2025, 2026 and HMMT Nov 2025 and Feb 2026),
Figure [1](#S1.F1 "Figure 1 ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")
compares these choices. When teacher scoring, student rollouts, and
evaluation use the same mode, Standard OPSD ([Zhao et al.,
2026a](#bib.bib5)) gives no sustained gains over Base, the starting
checkpoint. The largest sustained gains at 4B and 8B occur with a
Thinking-ON teacher, Thinking-OFF rollouts, and Thinking-OFF evaluation.
At Step 100 under Thinking-OFF evaluation, students trained with a
problem-only teacher have higher mean Avg@12 than those trained with a
privileged-solution teacher at all three scales (+1.36pp, +15.21pp, and
+8.60pp at 1.7B, 4B, and 8B). The 1.7B interval includes zero. Under
Thinking-ON evaluation, differences are small and mixed. We call this
pattern reasoning arbitrage: using a Thinking-ON teacher to improve
Thinking-OFF generation from the same model without a supplied solution.

Concurrent work questions both the source of OPSD gains and whether
privileged teacher targets improve student accuracy. OPSD gains can
survive replacing or removing the privileged solution ([Ichihara et al.,
2026](#bib.bib12); [Shrestha and Tessier, 2026](#bib.bib25); [Zhang et
al., 2026a](#bib.bib32)). Teacher targets can be pulled toward the
supplied solution rather than toward correctness ([Harne et al.,
2026](#bib.bib11)). Token-level scores need not track task success
([Nguyen et al., 2026](#bib.bib10)). Thinking models change behavior as
privileged context is withheld ([Kaur et al., 2026](#bib.bib8)).
Together, these studies leave open whether a teacher that solves with
the solution can teach the student to solve without it. We test the
steps required for transfer from the solution: the teacher must use it
(Source), the resulting token targets must be useful (Encoding), the
student must learn from those targets (Transmission), and the learned
behavior must appear without the solution (Expression). We examine these
steps across Qwen3 models using matched comparisons tailored to each
stage.

The Standard OPSD objective shows where the solution enters teacher
supervision while the student remains solution-free. For a minibatch
$`\{(x_{i},y_{i}^{\star})\}_{i\in\mathcal{B}}`$, the student $`p_{S}`$
(parameters $`\theta`$) samples
$`\hat{y}_{i}\sim p_{S}(\cdot\mid x_{i})`$. A frozen initial copy
$`p_{T}`$ alone sees $`y_{i}^{\star}`$ and supplies next-token targets
at the same prefixes. With $`D`$ denoting clipped forward KL, the
implemented loss averages over all valid response positions in the
minibatch:

|     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- |
|     |

````math
\begin{split}\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{OPSD}}(\theta;\mathcal{B})={}&\frac{1}{\sum_{i\in\mathcal{B}}|\hat{y}_{i}|}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{B}}\sum_{n=1}^{|\hat{y}_{i}|}D\big(p_{T}(\cdot\mid x_{i},y_{i}^{\star},\hat{y}_{i,<n})\,\big\|\,p_{S}(\cdot\mid x_{i},\hat{y}_{i,<n})\big).\end{split}
``` |  |

We organize the audit around four research questions. RQ1: Source
(§[4](#S4 "4 RQ1: Source ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models"))
Can the frozen teacher use the solution to solve hard problems, recover
after student errors, and recognize those errors? RQ2: Encoding
(§[5](#S5 "5 RQ2: Encoding ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models"))
Does adding the solution change token targets in ways that distinguish
correct from incorrect trajectories and help select continuations with
higher completion accuracy? RQ3: Transmission
(§[6](#S6 "6 RQ3: Transmission ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models"))
Does repeated OPSD make correct answers more frequent, including on
questions with no correct Base response in screening? Do gains require
the solution, and do they persist during longer training? RQ4:
Expression
(§[7](#S7 "7 RQ4: Expression ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models"))
Does training on verified corrections improve error recovery and solving
from the original question without the privileged solution?

To answer these questions, we audit privileged supervision from teacher
behavior to student generation. Across three Qwen3 scales on
mathematics, we test teacher solving and error recognition, token
targets, and student learning. We also test verified corrections at 4B
and 8B and run matched retention checks in OLMo and on coding tasks. The
results show that a teacher’s ability to use a solution does not
guarantee useful student supervision. The solution improves teacher
solving and recovery and shifts token targets, but does not reliably
improve error recognition, correctness ranking, or branch selection.
Repeated training raises pass@8 on the same questions, but Thinking-OFF
solving remains unreliable on audited questions with no correct Base
response in screening. Large Thinking-OFF gains occur with problem-only
teachers, and Purified OPSD ([Shen et al., 2026](#bib.bib9)) improves
retention during longer training. In the correction pilot, some variants
score slightly above Standard OPSD when generation starts from the
original question, but none improves error-prefix recovery and higher
correction doses can reduce broader accuracy. Together, these findings
support the reasoning-arbitrage interpretation of the largest gains.

## 2 Related Work

Privileged information and OPSD. Learning using privileged information
([Vapnik and Vashist, 2009](#bib.bib1); [Lopez-Paz et al.,
2016](#bib.bib2)) studies training-only supervision, while on-policy
distillation ([Agarwal et al., 2024](#bib.bib4)) evaluates teacher
targets on student-sampled states. OPSD combines them by conditioning a
frozen self-teacher on a privileged solution ([Zhao et al.,
2026a](#bib.bib5)). Related methods ([Shenfeld et al., 2026](#bib.bib6);
[Hübotter et al., 2026](#bib.bib7); [Ye et al., 2026](#bib.bib39)) use
demonstrations, feedback, or other training-only context. Variants alter
targets or credit assignment ([Shen et al., 2026](#bib.bib9); [Xu et
al., 2026](#bib.bib26); [Peng et al., 2026](#bib.bib27)). Concurrent
work varies the teacher’s context using problem-space guidance,
pseudo-solutions, or abstractions of full solutions ([Zhao et al.,
2026b](#bib.bib35); [Li et al., 2026a](#bib.bib36); [Tian et al.,
2026](#bib.bib38)), while Privileged Hidden Flow adds hidden-state
transition matching to output supervision ([Li et al.,
2026b](#bib.bib37)).

Empirical analyses of OPSD. At the target level, richer teacher context
can suppress epistemic verbalization ([Kim et al., 2026](#bib.bib33)),
privileged targets can penalize reconsideration tokens ([Kaur et al.,
2026](#bib.bib8)), and teacher targets can be pulled toward the supplied
solution ([Harne et al., 2026](#bib.bib11)). Privileged-likelihood
scores need not track correctness ([Nguyen et al., 2026](#bib.bib10)).
At the training level, gains can survive replacing or removing the
privileged solution ([Ichihara et al., 2026](#bib.bib12); [Shrestha and
Tessier, 2026](#bib.bib25)). Matched reference-free distillation finds
that the supplied reference explains only part of the improvement in
some settings ([Zhang et al., 2026a](#bib.bib32)). Full solutions need
not outperform abstracted teacher context ([Zhao et al.,
2026b](#bib.bib35); [Tian et al., 2026](#bib.bib38)). Separate analyses
find that OPSD can compress correct Thinking-ON rollouts more reliably
than it corrects incorrect ones ([Kim and Lee, 2026](#bib.bib31)) and
that a student can learn an average of instance-specific privileged
teachers that lacks needed information at test time ([Zhu et al.,
2026](#bib.bib34)). Our audit combines a teacher, rollout, and
evaluation thinking-mode factorial with matched problem-only controls.
It then tests teacher error recognition, correctness ranking and
sibling-branch selection, acquisition on questions with no correct Base
response in screening, and unaided generation after training on verified
corrections
(Appendix [A](#A1 "Appendix A Extended related work ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")).
These tests distinguish the solution’s contribution from the teacher’s
thinking-mode advantage and examine whether corrections learned with
privileged support appear in generation without it. Prior work on
self-correction without external feedback provides context for the
recognition test ([Huang et al., 2024](#bib.bib43); [Kamoi et al.,
2024](#bib.bib44)).

## 3 Experimental Setup

Models and data. Our primary audit uses Qwen3-1.7B, 4B, and 8B ([Yang et
al., 2025](#bib.bib13)). The main training runs use the mathematical
reasoning subset of OpenThoughts (OT) ([Guha et al., 2026](#bib.bib14)).
We also use Hendrycks MATH Level 5 (HM) ([Hendrycks et al.,
2021](#bib.bib17)) to audit hard problems and repeat selected analyses
on a second dataset. GPT-5.6-Sol checks whether each mathematical
problem is well posed and whether its answer and worked solution are
valid and consistent, without using model performance
(Appendix [B](#A2 "Appendix B Data and common setup ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")).
Qwen3’s chat template controls whether a dedicated thinking block is
enabled (Thinking ON) or disabled (Thinking OFF). Disabling the block
does not prevent visible step-by-step reasoning in the response. We set
the mode independently for teacher scoring, student rollouts, and
evaluation. Each teacher prompt uses the teacher’s selected mode
(Appendix [B.5](#A2.SS5 "B.5 Thinking modes and prompts ‣ Appendix B Data and common setup ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")).
Retention analyses also train OLMo-3-7B-Think ([Team Olmo,
2025](#bib.bib28)) on Hendrycks MATH and Qwen3-4B on execution-filtered
CodeContests ([Li et al., 2022](#bib.bib29)).

Training and controls. Our Standard OPSD baseline reimplements the
method of [Zhao et al. (2026a)](#bib.bib5) in veRL
(Appendix [B.4](#A2.SS4 "B.4 Training ‣ Appendix B Data and common setup ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")).
Unless noted, the Qwen3 student and its frozen teacher start from the
same Base checkpoint. Only the student’s LoRA parameters are updated.
Problem-only teachers receive the problem, while privileged teachers
also receive the worked solution
(Appendix [B.5](#A2.SS5 "B.5 Thinking modes and prompts ‣ Appendix B Data and common setup ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")).
Teacher and student thinking modes may differ. Each primary run
processes the same 3,200 OT examples once, with 100 updates at global
batch size 32. For each problem, the student samples one response of up
to 1,024 tokens. The frozen teacher and current student score the same
sampled response prefixes under their respective inputs. The
teacher-to-student forward-KL (FKL) loss caps each vocabulary term at
0.05 before summing over vocabulary and averages over valid response
tokens across the global minibatch. We use this cap at every scale and
in both thinking modes. Unless noted, training runs use three seeds
matched across conditions.

| Model |  Problem only  |  Shuffled      |  Privileged      |
|-------|----------------|----------------|------------------|
| 1.7B  |  2.93          |  1.13 (-1.80)  |  40.54 (+37.61)  |
| 4B    |  1.14          |  1.70 (+0.57)  |  64.20 (+63.07)  |
| 8B    |  0.00          |  1.43 (+1.43)  |  58.57 (+58.57)  |

Table 1: Privileged solutions improve hard-problem solving. Avg@4 (%) on
HM problems with no correct screening rollout. Parentheses give changes
from problem only (pp). Appendix
Table [D6](#A4.SS2.SSS2 "D.2.2 Hard-problem solving ‣ D.2 Hendrycks MATH Level 5: solving and replications ‣ Appendix D RQ1: Source ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")
reports paired 95% question-bootstrap intervals.

| Model |  Problem only  |  Shuffled        |  Privileged      |
|-------|----------------|------------------|------------------|
| 1.7B  |  48.50         |  41.50 (-7.00)   |  74.50 (+26.00)  |
| 4B    |  66.00         |  51.00 (-15.00)  |  88.50 (+22.50)  |
| 8B    |  63.00         |  53.50 (-9.50)   |  88.50 (+25.50)  |

Table 2: Privileged solutions improve post-error recovery. Avg@4 (%) on
50 verified OT error prefixes per scale. Parentheses give changes from
problem only (pp). Appendix
Table [D2](#A4.SS1.SSS2 "D.1.2 Recovery after errors ‣ D.1 OpenThoughts: recovery and recognition ‣ Appendix D RQ1: Source ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")
reports paired 95% cluster-bootstrap intervals.

Evaluation. On the five mathematics benchmarks, we evaluate without the
privileged solution. We draw 12 responses per problem with a
38,912-token cap and score extracted final answers with an automatic
mathematical-equivalence verifier. We report mean Avg@12 across AIME
2024–2026 and HMMT Nov 2025 and Feb 2026 ([Zhang and Math-AI Team,
2024](#bib.bib18); [MathArena, 2025a](#bib.bib19); [MathArena,
2026a](#bib.bib20); [MathArena, 2025b](#bib.bib21); [MathArena,
2026b](#bib.bib22)). Coding evaluations also omit the privileged
solution and use four execution-scored responses per problem on
LiveCodeBench v6 ([Jain et al., 2025](#bib.bib30)) and held-out
CodeContests ([Li et al., 2022](#bib.bib29)).
Appendix [B.6](#A2.SS6 "B.6 Evaluation ‣ Appendix B Data and common setup ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")
gives further details.

## 4 RQ1: Source

RQ1 tests whether the teacher can use the privileged solution to solve
hard problems, recover from student errors, and recognize those errors.
We run the teacher with Thinking ON, the teacher mode used in our main
Standard OPSD comparisons and in every configuration with sustained
gains in
Figure [1](#S1.F1 "Figure 1 ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models").
We keep the checkpoint fixed and vary only its context, isolating how
privileged information affects solving, recovery, and error recognition
in these audits.

### 4.1 Solution improves hard-problem solving

For each model scale, we select hard problems from the HM dataset with
no correct response among 12 screening rollouts, as confirmed by
GPT-5.6-Sol and Claude Opus 4.8 after an automatic answer-equivalence
screen. From the 2,242 cleaned questions
(§[3](#S3 "3 Experimental Setup ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")),
this yields 111 (5.0%), 44 (2.0%), and 35 (1.6%) questions at 1.7B, 4B,
and 8B. On each question, the teacher generates four fresh rollouts
under each of three prompt configurations: problem only, a worked
solution from another problem (shuffled reference), or the solution to
the current problem (privileged solution). Because these are new
samples, problem-only accuracy need not be zero. The two judges also
assess these fresh responses at the task level, with disagreements
resolved by a separate blinded pass.
Appendix [D.2](#A4.SS2 "D.2 Hendrycks MATH Level 5: solving and replications ‣ Appendix D RQ1: Source ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")
gives the protocol.

Privileged-solution input substantially improves the teacher’s
task-level accuracy, while a shuffled reference offers almost no
advantage
(Table [2](#S3.T2 "Table 2 ‣ 3 Experimental Setup ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")).
A blind audit of the privileged-solution responses finds that 33.3% of
confirmed improvements closely reproduce the supplied solution, and only
45.7% are judged self-contained
(Appendix [D.2.5](#A4.SS2.SSS5 "D.2.5 Blind response audit ‣ D.2 Hendrycks MATH Level 5: solving and replications ‣ Appendix D RQ1: Source ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")).
In the Thinking-ON OT audit, the corresponding rates are 10.0% and 87.5%
(Appendix [D](#A4 "Appendix D RQ1: Source ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")).
In this HM audit, more than half of confirmed improvements depend on the
supplied solution rather than giving a self-contained derivation.

### 4.2 Solution improves recovery after errors

To test recovery after a student error, we first segment OT Base
trajectories into 128-token chunks. GPT-5.6-Sol and Claude Opus 4.8 each
mark the earliest chunk containing a mathematical error. We retain 50
trajectories per model scale on which both judges identify an error.
Each is cut after the later marked chunk, so that the final chunk of
each prompt contains the error. The teacher generates four completions
per prompt in each of the same three context conditions. The two judges
independently assess each completion, with disagreements resolved by a
separate blinded pass. We count recovery only if it repairs or abandons
the error and reaches a valid solution
(Appendix [D.1.2](#A4.SS1.SSS2 "D.1.2 Recovery after errors ‣ D.1 OpenThoughts: recovery and recognition ‣ Appendix D RQ1: Source ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")).

Table [2](#S3.T2 "Table 2 ‣ 3 Experimental Setup ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")
shows that the privileged solution raises Avg@4 by between 22.5 and 26.0
percentage points over problem only and by between 33.0 and 37.5 points
over shuffled references. Every paired 95% interval excludes zero
(Appendix
Table [D2](#A4.SS1.SSS2 "D.1.2 Recovery after errors ‣ D.1 OpenThoughts: recovery and recognition ‣ Appendix D RQ1: Source ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")).
Because shuffled references underperform problem-only inputs, extra
solution-like text does not explain the privileged gain. The added
recovery benefit depends on task-matched information. The solution
therefore remains actionable after a verified error, but recovery does
not show that the teacher identified the error rather than re-solving
from the supplied solution.
Section [4.3](#S4.SS3 "4.3 Error recognition remains limited ‣ 4 RQ1: Source ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")
tests this distinction directly.

### 4.3 Error recognition remains limited

{wrapfigure}

r0.52

Privileged solutions do not reliably improve error recognition. Accuracy
(%) on 200 OT pairs of valid and erroneous prefixes per scale, with and
without the privileged solution. “Both correct” requires both judgments
in a pair to be correct. Paired 95% intervals are in Appendix
Table [D3](#A4.SS1.SSS3 "D.1.3 Error recognition ‣ D.1 OpenThoughts: recovery and recognition ‣ Appendix D RQ1: Source ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models").

Recovery alone does not show that the teacher identifies the error: it
could solve the problem again using the supplied solution. To test
explicit verbal recognition directly, as distinct from the implicit
token-level signal that OPSD trains on, we use a separate set of 200
audited OT prefix pairs per scale. Each pair comes from the same
trajectory and contains a valid prefix ending before either judge’s
marked error chunk and an erroneous prefix ending after both. The
teacher judges whether each prefix is correct so far, with and without
the privileged solution. We report accuracy on valid and erroneous
prefixes, plus pair accuracy, which requires both judgments to be
correct
(Appendix [D.1.3](#A4.SS1.SSS3 "D.1.3 Error recognition ‣ D.1 OpenThoughts: recovery and recognition ‣ Appendix D RQ1: Source ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")).

With the privileged solution, the teacher correctly flags only between
13.0% and 25.5% of erroneous prefixes, lower than with problem only at
4B and 8B
(Figure [4.3](#S4.SS3 "4.3 Error recognition remains limited ‣ 4 RQ1: Source ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")).
Pair accuracy ranges from 6.5% to 13.0%, and the paired 95% confidence
interval for the difference from problem only includes zero at every
scale. Overall prefix accuracy, averaged over valid and erroneous
prefixes, shows a gain whose paired 95% interval excludes zero only at
1.7B (+5.75pp). Even there, accuracy remains below the 50% baseline of
accepting every prefix
(Appendix [D.1.3](#A4.SS1.SSS3 "D.1.3 Error recognition ‣ D.1 OpenThoughts: recovery and recognition ‣ Appendix D RQ1: Source ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")).
The response parse rate ranges from 78.8% to 97.0%, and unparsed
responses count as incorrect.

## 5 RQ2: Encoding

RQ2 tests whether the privileged solution changes the teacher’s
token-level supervision and whether that change tracks downstream gains,
favors correct trajectories, or helps select better continuations.

### 5.1 The solution changes the teacher’s targets

|  Trajectory  |  Teacher  |  1.7B   |  4B     |  8B     |
|--------------|-----------|---------|---------|---------|
|  OFF         |  OFF      |  0.051  |  0.037  |  0.033  |
|  OFF         |  ON       |  0.042  |  0.035  |  0.031  |
|  ON          |  OFF      |  0.138  |  0.104  |  0.099  |
|  ON          |  ON       |  0.076  |  0.069  |  0.058  |

Table 3: Privileged solutions increase FKL across scales and thinking
modes. $`\Delta\text{FKL}_{\text{ref}}`$ on fixed Base trajectories.
OFF/ON denote trajectory and teacher thinking. Appendix
Table [E1a](#A5.SS1.SSS1 "E.1.1 Construction and scoring ‣ E.1 Target shift ‣ Appendix E RQ2: Encoding ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")
reports paired 95% confidence intervals.

| Model | States |  Student selected  |  Privileged selected  |
|-------|--------|--------------------|-----------------------|
| 1.7B  | 149    |  57.38             |  63.09 (+5.70)        |
| 4B    | 150    |  73.67             |  74.33 (+0.67)        |
| 8B    | 150    |  80.50             |  79.33 (-1.17)        |

Table 4: Privileged selection improves success reliably only at 1.7B.
Avg@4 (%) over four completions per selected branch from verified OT
error states. Parentheses show changes from student selection (pp).
Appendix
Table [E5](#A5.SS3.SSS2 "E.3.2 Cohort construction and adjudication ‣ E.3 Branch choice ‣ Appendix E RQ2: Encoding ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")
reports paired 95% state-bootstrap intervals.

We score fixed correct-incorrect Base trajectory pairs per question
using teachers with and without the privileged solution. We define the
change in forward KL as
$`\Delta\text{FKL}_{\text{ref}}=\text{FKL}(\text{privileged})-\text{FKL}(\text{problem only})`$,
where each FKL measures teacher-to-student divergence over the same
fixed prefixes. This quantity captures how privileged conditioning
changes the divergence, not the distance between teacher targets.
Thinking-OFF trajectories come from OT and Thinking-ON trajectories from
HM. Each has at least 1,024 clean response tokens, and we score a
512-token suffix. Because the modes come from different corpora, their
difference should be interpreted descriptively rather than as a
controlled estimate of thinking-mode effects.

As shown in
Table [4](#S5.T4 "Table 4 ‣ 5.1 The solution changes the teacher’s targets ‣ 5 RQ2: Encoding ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models"),
adding the solution increases FKL in all 12 settings. We focus on the
Thinking-ON teacher used during training. Adding the solution produces
nearly twice the FKL increase on trajectories generated by a Thinking-ON
student compared with those generated by a Thinking-OFF student
(1.80–1.97 times across model scales). The same pattern holds when both
trajectory modes use the same HM questions (Appendix
Table [E2](#A5.SS1.SSS2 "E.1.2 Hendrycks MATH Level 5 replication ‣ E.1 Target shift ‣ Appendix E RQ2: Encoding ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")).
Yet the largest sustained benchmark gains come from training on
Thinking-OFF rollouts, which have the smaller FKL changes
(Figure [1](#S1.F1 "Figure 1 ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")).
A larger FKL change is therefore not a reliable indicator of a larger
performance gain.

### 5.2 Correctness ranking remains inconsistent

{wrapfigure}

r0.49

The solution does not consistently improve correctness ranking.
Exact-FKL AUROC for ranking incorrect above correct trajectories.
Trained checkpoints average three seeds. Problem-only AUROC is 0.5 at
Step 0.
Appendix [E.2](#A5.SS2 "E.2 Correctness ranking ‣ Appendix E RQ2: Encoding ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")
gives cohorts and clipped-loss values.

We test whether incorrect trajectories exhibit larger teacher-to-student
divergence than correct ones, using exact FKL as a diagnostic of
correctness sensitivity. We treat the incorrect trajectory as the
positive class, so an AUROC above 0.5 indicates larger divergence on
incorrect trajectories. This score measures distributional disagreement.
It does not directly measure gradient strength or the usefulness of an
update.

We reuse the correct-incorrect trajectory pairs from
§[5.1](#S5.SS1 "5.1 The solution changes the teacher’s targets ‣ 5 RQ2: Encoding ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models"),
keeping only those whose labels two judges (GPT-5.6-Sol and Claude Opus
4.8) confirm. For each trajectory and each checkpoint, we average the
per-token exact FKL over a fixed 512-token suffix, computed between the
frozen Thinking-ON teacher and the current student checkpoint. Comparing
AUROC with and without the privileged solution isolates what the
solution adds
(Appendix [E.2](#A5.SS2 "E.2 Correctness ranking ‣ Appendix E RQ2: Encoding ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")).

Results are shown in
Figure [5.2](#S5.SS2 "5.2 Correctness ranking remains inconsistent ‣ 5 RQ2: Encoding ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models").
Overall, AUROC is close to 0.5 in most settings, with a temporary
advantage for Qwen3-4B on Thinking-OFF trajectories. Hence, the gains
shown in
Figure [1](#S1.F1 "Figure 1 ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")
do not correspond to a persistent privilege-specific correctness
ranking. Trajectory-level ranking is a coarse diagnostic rather than a
necessary condition for useful token-local learning, so we conclude only
that this FKL readout does not consistently separate correct from
incorrect trajectories.

\WFclear

### 5.3 Branch selection improves only at 1.7B

Finally, we test whether the privileged solution helps select a better
continuation at a state just before a student error. At each OT prefix
ending before an error verified by GPT-5.6-Sol and Claude Opus 4.8, the
student generates four 128-token branches. We compare branches selected
by the student’s mean log likelihood with those selected by the
difference between the privileged teacher’s and student’s mean log
likelihoods, estimating each branch’s success as Avg@4 over four
completions
(Appendix [E.3](#A5.SS3 "E.3 Branch choice ‣ Appendix E RQ2: Encoding ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")).
In a stratified audit of continuations marked incorrect by the automatic
verifier, judges deem between 16.4% and 64.6% of non-ambiguous cases
correct across model scales (Appendix
Table [E7](#A5.SS3.SSS2 "E.3.2 Cohort construction and adjudication ‣ E.3 Branch choice ‣ Appendix E RQ2: Encoding ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")).
We therefore use blinded task-level adjudication for all selector
outcomes
(Appendix [E.3.2](#A5.SS3.SSS2 "E.3.2 Cohort construction and adjudication ‣ E.3 Branch choice ‣ Appendix E RQ2: Encoding ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")).

Table [4](#S5.T4 "Table 4 ‣ 5.1 The solution changes the teacher’s targets ‣ 5 RQ2: Encoding ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")
shows a reliable advantage for the privileged selector only at 1.7B on
OT (+5.70pp). At 4B and 8B on OT, the differences are +0.67pp and
-1.17pp, and both paired intervals include zero. HM shows no robust
effect at any model scale (Appendix
Tables [E5](#A5.SS3.SSS2 "E.3.2 Cohort construction and adjudication ‣ E.3 Branch choice ‣ Appendix E RQ2: Encoding ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")
and [E6](#A5.SS3.SSS2 "E.3.2 Cohort construction and adjudication ‣ E.3 Branch choice ‣ Appendix E RQ2: Encoding ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")).
The tested privileged selector therefore shows no consistent advantage
across scales and corpora.

## 6 RQ3: Transmission

RQ3 examines what the student learns from OPSD. We test repeated
training on the same questions, the contribution of privileged
solutions, and whether gains persist during longer training.

### 6.1 Repeated training raises pass@8

Figure 2: Pass@8 gains from repeated training. Change from each curve’s
Base on its own OT set. Thinking OFF and ON indicate matched student
training and evaluation modes. Curves are three-seed means with
seed-range shading.

![Refer to caption](2611.16064v1/05_privilege_interface.png)

Figure 3: Large Thinking-OFF gains occur with both teacher inputs. A
Thinking-ON teacher supervises Thinking-OFF rollouts. Columns vary
teacher input. Rows vary evaluation mode and scale. U/F denote
Unsolved/Frontier. Cells show three-seed $`\Delta`$Avg@8 (pp) from Base.

We train separately on OT Unsolved (0/12 correct Base responses) and
Frontier (1–5/12) questions for about 16 epochs, testing whether
repeated training helps on questions with no observed Base success or
mainly makes occasional successes more frequent. These runs use a
Thinking-ON privileged-solution teacher and report pass@8
(Figure [3](#S6.F3 "Figure 3 ‣ 6.1 Repeated training raises pass@8 ‣ 6 RQ3: Transmission ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")).
Figures [3](#S6.F3 "Figure 3 ‣ 6.1 Repeated training raises pass@8 ‣ 6 RQ3: Transmission ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")
and [3](#S6.F3 "Figure 3 ‣ 6.1 Repeated training raises pass@8 ‣ 6 RQ3: Transmission ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")
evaluate the training questions, so they measure question-level fitting.
Base pass@8 is 63–83% on Frontier but only 7–24% on Unsolved, and the
two modes use different question sets. Because a 0/12 label can reflect
missed successes or grading errors, we also train on a separate 0/16 set
whose 16 rollouts both judges confirm incorrect: 60, 24, and 11
questions at 1.7B, 4B, and 8B, respectively
(Appendix [F.1.6](#A6.SS1.SSS6 "F.1.6 Questions with no correct Base responses ‣ F.1 OpenThoughts acquisition and thinking-mode audit ‣ Appendix F RQ3: Transmission ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")).

Repeated training increases pass@8 on both question sets
(Figure [3](#S6.F3 "Figure 3 ‣ 6.1 Repeated training raises pass@8 ‣ 6 RQ3: Transmission ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")).
The largest gains occur on Unsolved questions under Thinking OFF:
25.4–47.9pp after 16 epochs, versus 11.6–13.1pp under Thinking ON, so
the student benefits from a Thinking-ON teacher with its own thinking
block disabled. However, Thinking-OFF solving stays unreliable on the
0/16 set: at the final checkpoints, Avg@16 is only 3.2% to 7.2%, and no
question reaches 14/16 correct responses with either teacher input,
while the same checkpoints reach 61.7% to 78.2% under Thinking ON
(Appendix
Table [F1g](#A6.SS1.SSS6 "F.1.6 Questions with no correct Base responses ‣ F.1 OpenThoughts acquisition and thinking-mode audit ‣ Appendix F RQ3: Transmission ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")).
These questions are unsolved under Thinking OFF, not globally, and 16
epochs on these small sets are only about 30, 12, and 6 optimizer
updates. OPSD thus sharpens the output distribution toward correct
samples, but within so few updates we do not observe transfer of
Thinking-ON success to Thinking OFF.

### 6.2 Gains do not require the solution

To separate the privileged solution from the thinking mode, we fix
teacher and rollout modes, question sets, schedule, and seeds and vary
only whether the teacher receives the solution.

As shown in
Figure [3](#S6.F3 "Figure 3 ‣ 6.1 Repeated training raises pass@8 ‣ 6 RQ3: Transmission ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models"),
after 16 epochs, teachers improve Thinking-OFF Avg@8 with or without the
privileged solution. In fact, the problem-only teacher gives the larger
Thinking-OFF gain in four of six scale and question-set comparisons. The
pattern holds on held-out benchmarks: at 4B and 8B, problem-only
teachers raise Thinking-OFF Avg@12 by 15.98–22.70pp, versus 5.68–10.66pp
for privileged-solution teachers (Appendix
Table [F1f](#A6.SS1.SSS5 "F.1.5 Held-out transfer ‣ F.1 OpenThoughts acquisition and thinking-mode audit ‣ Appendix F RQ3: Transmission ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")).
In the one-pass runs of
Figure [1](#S1.F1 "Figure 1 ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")b,
the privileged-solution teacher leads by 1.02pp to 1.91pp at Step 20
under Thinking-OFF evaluation, but this relative advantage reverses by
Step 100 (Appendix
Table [C3](#A3.SS2 "C.2 Figure b: privileged-solution versus problem-only teacher ‣ Appendix C Figure : complete results ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")).
For Thinking-ON student training, the problem-only teacher and student
receive identical inputs and start with identical weights, so KL is zero
and no update occurs. Adding the solution improves training-set Avg@8 by
2.8–7.4pp after 16 epochs (Appendix
Figure [F1](#A6.SS1.SSS3 "F.1.3 Privilege and thinking-mode controls ‣ F.1 OpenThoughts acquisition and thinking-mode audit ‣ Appendix F RQ3: Transmission ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")).
A Thinking-ON teacher can therefore improve Thinking-OFF generation
without a supplied solution.

### 6.3 Purified OPSD limits late decline

{wrapfigure}

r0.5

Purified OPSD limits the late decline seen with Standard OPSD. (a) Qwen3
mathematics, including runs that switch between methods. (b) OLMo with
exact FKL fixed and only teacher targets changed. (c–d) Qwen3-4B coding
accuracy and truncation.

To test whether gains survive longer training, we compare Standard OPSD
with Purified OPSD ([Shen et al., 2026](#bib.bib9)). Purified discounts
solution-only teacher signal and anchors its target to the problem-only
Base model using three frozen-model passes and a Jensen–Shannon loss
(Appendix [F.2.1](#A6.SS2.SSS1 "F.2.1 Purified target ‣ F.2 Purification and retention ‣ Appendix F RQ3: Transmission ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")).
Teacher and student rollouts both use Thinking ON here, a setting in
which a problem-only teacher yields no update. At Step 100, Purified
scores 0.74–1.86pp above Base across Qwen3 scales, while Standard scores
1.92–3.57pp below it. Purified’s gain is therefore evidence that
solution-conditioned targets can improve held-out accuracy. Standard
OPSD does not reliably deliver that signal. Switching from Standard to
Purified recovers accuracy. The reverse switch ends below Base
(Figure [6.3](#S6.SS3 "6.3 Purified OPSD limits late decline ‣ 6 RQ3: Transmission ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")a
and
Appendix [F.2](#A6.SS2 "F.2 Purification and retention ‣ Appendix F RQ3: Transmission ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")).

The OLMo comparison holds exact FKL fixed for both raw and purified
targets, isolating target construction
(Figure [6.3](#S6.SS3 "6.3 Purified OPSD limits late decline ‣ 6 RQ3: Transmission ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")b).
At Step 100, the purified target reaches 58.52% Avg@12, above both the
raw target at 52.35% and Base at 55.76%. The purified target outperforms
the raw target in all three seeds, so this advantage does not require
replacing FKL with the Jensen–Shannon loss used in the main Purified
runs.

On Qwen3-4B coding, Standard improves early but then loses accuracy as
truncation rises. At Step 100, Purified exceeds Standard by 4.45pp on
LiveCodeBench v6 and 6.57pp on CodeContests, while its truncation rates
remain near Base
(Figure [6.3](#S6.SS3 "6.3 Purified OPSD limits late decline ‣ 6 RQ3: Transmission ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")c–d).
The ordering persists with a longer coding rollout budget and in a
single-seed Qwen3-4B mathematics continuation to 200 updates
(Appendix [F.2](#A6.SS2 "F.2 Purification and retention ‣ Appendix F RQ3: Transmission ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")).

## 7 RQ4: Expression

RQ4 asks whether training on verified corrections helps the student
repair errors without the privileged solution. From mixed-outcome OT
questions, where the Thinking-ON Base produces at least one correct and
one incorrect trajectory, GPT-5.6-Sol marks a local error in each
incorrect trajectory and writes a repair instruction. From that error
prefix, the teacher generates corrections given the privileged solution
and the instruction, and we keep verifier-correct, self-contained ones.

{wrapfigure}

r0.55

Training on verified corrections does not consistently improve
generation accuracy. Accuracy on held-out questions starting (a) from
the audited error prefix or (b) from the original problem, without the
solution. Three-seed means. Dashed lines mark Base.

At Qwen3-4B and 8B, we split the correction dataset (125 and 102
questions) into training and held-out sets by question, holding out 25
and 20 questions. Frozen-Base accuracy on the held-out questions is
already 86–92% and each question is worth 4–5pp, so this is a small,
near-ceiling pilot in which correction batches replace, rather than add
to, Standard OPSD updates. Correction25, Correction50, and Correction100
replace 25%, 50%, or 100% of the Standard OPSD updates with supervised
fine-tuning (SFT) on correction tokens. We report held-out Avg@8 when
continuing from the audited error prefix
(Figure [7](#S7 "7 RQ4: Expression ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")a)
or solving the original question
(Figure [7](#S7 "7 RQ4: Expression ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")b).

Held-out NLL on the correction targets falls monotonically with
correction dose (Appendix
Tables [G4a](#A7.SS4 "G.4 Step-48 generation endpoints ‣ Appendix G RQ4: Expression ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")–[G4b](#A7.SS4 "G.4 Step-48 generation endpoints ‣ Appendix G RQ4: Expression ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")),
so the student does learn to predict the corrections. Yet after 48
updates, all three correction variants have lower Avg@8 than Standard
OPSD when generation starts from the error prefix. Starting from the
original question, Correction25 and Correction50 at 4B and Correction100
at 8B are slightly above Standard OPSD, but no variant consistently
outperforms it, and Correction100 lowers five-benchmark Avg@12 by 7.25pp
at 4B and 2.42pp at 8B relative to Base. A same-prefix control at 4B
attributes this decline to the correction continuations
(Appendix [G.5](#A7.SS5 "G.5 Matched controls ‣ Appendix G RQ4: Expression ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")).

\WFclear

## 8 Discussion

Together, the four audits show that the solution’s effect on the frozen
teacher does not map directly onto student performance. It improves
teacher solving and recovery, but its token-level changes do not
reliably favor correct trajectories. Student gains can occur without the
solution, while learning to predict verified corrections does not
consistently improve unaided repair. We now use additional controls to
assess what privileged training contributes and why gains sometimes
decline with continued training.

Figure 4: Weight mixing, parameterization, and token-selection
diagnostics. (a–b) Weights mixed between problem-only and
privileged-solution runs, including a 50% extrapolation past the
privileged endpoint. (c) LoRA versus full-parameter training. (d) Token
selectors over 135 branch events, with 95% intervals. (a–c) show
three-seed means. Dashed lines mark the frozen Base checkpoint from
which each run begins.

Teacher success does not guarantee student gains. To examine what the
solution adds, we mix weights from matched problem-only and
privileged-solution Standard OPSD runs at Qwen3-4B. At Step 100, the
privileged endpoint and extrapolation beyond it score below problem-only
weights under Thinking OFF, and slightly below under Thinking ON
(Figure [4](#S8.F4 "Figure 4 ‣ 8 Discussion ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")a,b).
The privileged endpoint therefore offers no accuracy advantage in this
comparison. The early gains and Purified improvements in RQ3 show that
this late-training result does not extend to every use of
solution-conditioned targets.

The largest gains are consistent with reasoning arbitrage. Problem-only
teachers often match or exceed privileged-solution teachers (RQ3),
showing that gains need not depend on privileged solutions. On verified
Unsolved questions, the final checkpoints after approximately 16 epochs
reach only 3.2% to 7.2% Thinking-OFF Avg@16, compared with 61.7% to
78.2% under Thinking ON (Appendix
Table [F1g](#A6.SS1.SSS6 "F.1.6 Questions with no correct Base responses ‣ F.1 OpenThoughts acquisition and thinking-mode audit ‣ Appendix F RQ3: Transmission ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")).
These questions remain difficult under the Thinking-OFF interface
despite substantial success under Thinking ON. We hypothesize that some
gains arise from making Thinking-ON behavior easier to elicit under
Thinking OFF, resembling the compression role of [Kim and Lee
(2026)](#bib.bib31). The mechanism underlying this pattern remains
unresolved.

The late decline persists beyond the default training settings. Standard
OPSD ends below Base with full-parameter training
(Figure [4](#S8.F4 "Figure 4 ‣ 8 Discussion ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")c),
unclipped FKL, and longer rollouts (Appendix
Tables [H1b](#A8.SS2 "H.2 Diagnostic values ‣ Appendix H Discussion diagnostics ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")
and [H1d](#A8.SS2 "H.2 Diagnostic values ‣ Appendix H Discussion diagnostics ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")).
These controls show that the decline persists across the tested
settings, although the clipping and rollout controls use a single
training seed. The matched exact-FKL OLMo comparison (RQ3) further shows
that changing target construction can improve retention while keeping
the loss fixed.

Choosing useful targets and applying corrections require separate tests.
At Qwen3-4B, a hindsight oracle choosing between the student’s and the
target’s preferred next token yields an estimated 11.30pp gain over 135
audited branch events, yet a held-out critic ranks near chance with
pooled AUROC 0.452
(Figure [4](#S8.F4 "Figure 4 ‣ 8 Discussion ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")d).
FKL-selected sparse replay also shows no clear advantage over matched
random replay in the single-seed pilot
(Appendix [H.3](#A8.SS3 "H.3 Action selection ‣ Appendix H Discussion diagnostics ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")).
Useful local alternatives therefore do not by themselves establish
reliable gains from selection or subsequent training. In RQ4, lower
correction NLL likewise does not consistently improve recovery during
generation. Target availability, selection, retention, and generation
should be evaluated separately. Evaluations should also compare runs
differing only in whether the teacher receives the solution, and
evaluate Base and trained students under both thinking modes.

## 9 Conclusion

We audit how worked solutions shape supervision and student behavior in
OPSD across three Qwen3 scales. Privileged solutions improve teacher
solving and recovery, but do not reliably improve error recognition or
correctness-sensitive token supervision. Learned corrections likewise do
not consistently improve student recovery during generation. The largest
gains at 4B and 8B recur with problem-only teachers and track the
teacher’s thinking mode. Purified OPSD better preserves accuracy,
demonstrating that solution-conditioned training can still yield
benefits. Together, these findings show that teacher competence and
student accuracy gains alone do not establish transfer from privileged
solutions. Evaluations should isolate what the solution adds through
matched problem-only controls, assess both thinking modes, and test
whether improvements persist through training and appear in unaided
generation.

first situates the audit relative to concurrent work.

documents data preparation and the common training, prompt, and

provide the full Source, Encoding, Transmission, and Expression

## Appendix A Extended related work

Privileged information and on-policy self-distillation. Learning using
privileged information studies supervision that is available during
training but absent at deployment ([Vapnik and Vashist,
2009](#bib.bib1)), and generalized distillation unifies it with
knowledge distillation ([Lopez-Paz et al., 2016](#bib.bib2); [Hinton et
al., 2015](#bib.bib3)). On-policy distillation evaluates teacher targets
on states sampled by the student rather than on fixed demonstrations
([Agarwal et al., 2024](#bib.bib4)). OPSD applies this idea with a
frozen copy of the student conditioned on a privileged solution ([Zhao
et al., 2026a](#bib.bib5)). Closely related formulations use a
demonstration-conditioned EMA teacher for continual learning, an
evolving feedback-conditioned teacher for reinforcement learning, a
context-conditioned teacher for on-policy context distillation, or
jointly trained privileged and unprivileged views for agent training
([Shenfeld et al., 2026](#bib.bib6); [Hübotter et al., 2026](#bib.bib7);
[Ye et al., 2026](#bib.bib39); [Penaloza et al., 2026](#bib.bib40)).
Recent variants alter the target or its credit assignment: Purified OPSD
subtracts a reference-only component ([Shen et al., 2026](#bib.bib9)),
$`\beta`$-OPSD interpolates between a reference policy and the
privileged teacher and adds return-to-go credit ([Xu et al.,
2026](#bib.bib26)), and AD-OPSD softly anchors high-risk tokens to a
frozen base-model prior ([Peng et al., 2026](#bib.bib27)). Concurrent
work also changes the teacher’s privileged view or the supervision
channel. PS-OPSD replaces a full solution with problem-space guidance
([Zhao et al., 2026b](#bib.bib35)). U-OPSD constructs pseudo-solutions
from self-consistency ([Li et al., 2026a](#bib.bib36)). Privileged
Hidden Flow adds hidden-state transition matching ([Li et al.,
2026b](#bib.bib37)). Context-design experiments compare full solutions
with answer-only and multiple abstraction levels ([Tian et al.,
2026](#bib.bib38)). Broader extensions select verified teachers across
domains or apply privileged self-distillation to multi-turn GUI
interaction ([He et al., 2026](#bib.bib41); [Zhang et al.,
2026b](#bib.bib42)). We use Purified and $`\beta`$-OPSD as matched
diagnostic controls rather than as a method leaderboard
(§[6.3](#S6.SS3 "6.3 Purified OPSD limits late decline ‣ 6 RQ3: Transmission ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")
and
Appendix [H](#A8 "Appendix H Discussion diagnostics ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")).

Source. [Kaur et al. (2026)](#bib.bib8) directly evaluate the
gold-demonstration-conditioned model, whose pass@k remains high even as
its rollouts shorten. RQ1 separately tests whether the frozen privileged
teacher can solve problems that the Base model fails, repair audited
erroneous student prefixes, and recognize where errors occur, using
matched problem-only, shuffled-reference, and privileged-solution
inputs.

Encoding. [Kim et al. (2026)](#bib.bib33) trace self-distillation
degradation to suppressed epistemic verbalization when the teacher
conditions on rich information. [Kaur et al. (2026)](#bib.bib8) find
that privileged context degrades thinking models more at long budgets
and with a full solution than with the final answer alone, and that
privileged teachers penalize sampled reconsideration tokens. [Harne et
al. (2026)](#bib.bib11) show that loss falls while accuracy stays flat
or declines, tracing this to teacher targets pulled toward the supplied
reference and to per-token losses that do not separate correct from
incorrect rollouts. [Nguyen et al. (2026)](#bib.bib10) distinguish
privileged-likelihood change from action value: on AIME 2025 with
rollout-derived feedback, token scores discriminate correct from
incorrect rollouts at near-chance level and training trails outcome-only
reinforcement learning, whereas on SciKnowEval Biology score
discrimination and learning agree. Concurrent context studies find that
structured or abstracted guidance can outperform full solutions and that
initial teacher-to-student KL does not order downstream utility ([Zhao
et al., 2026b](#bib.bib35); [Tian et al., 2026](#bib.bib38)). RQ2
complements these analyses by measuring target shift on identical
tokens, correctness ranking across checkpoints, and sibling-branch
selection at audited error states.

Transmission. [Ichihara et al. (2026)](#bib.bib12) replace the
privileged solution with a problem and solution from another example and
find that gains remain competitive with OPSD. [Shrestha and Tessier
(2026)](#bib.bib25) find that students improve without the correct
reference and that another problem’s solution can outperform it on
several benchmarks. U-OPSD further constructs its teacher context
without ground-truth supervision ([Li et al., 2026a](#bib.bib36)). [Kaur
et al. (2026)](#bib.bib8) observe that disabling thinking during OPSD
training preserves Base accuracy. [Kim and Lee (2026)](#bib.bib31) find
that in thinking-enabled mathematics OPSD acts more as compression than
correction: training only on correct rollouts preserves accuracy while
shortening responses, whereas training only on incorrect rollouts lowers
it. [Zhu et al. (2026)](#bib.bib34) attribute OPSD failures in
mathematics to the test-time absence of instance-specific privileged
information, which leads the student to learn a
privileged-information-free average of teachers. Concurrently, [Zhang et
al. (2026a)](#bib.bib32) report that reference-free distillation from a
thinking-enabled teacher accounts for much of the gain on
direct-response rollouts. RQ3 adds a full teacher, rollout, and
evaluation factorial, a direct matched privileged-versus-problem-only
contrast
(Table [C3](#A3.SS2 "C.2 Figure b: privileged-solution versus problem-only teacher ‣ Appendix C Figure : complete results ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")),
strict Base-zero acquisition tests under repeated exposure, and
retention checks across model families and domains.

Expression. [Kaur et al. (2026)](#bib.bib8) count deliberation markers
in free generation after training, but, to our knowledge, none of the
analyses above tests whether corrections the student has been trained to
predict are used without privileged support. RQ4 separates
teacher-forced fit from unsupported and cue-supported use under matched
controls.

## Appendix B Data and common setup

We audit the two mathematical source pools used to construct training
sets or model-relative difficulty cohorts before inspecting any
experimental outcome. GPT-5.6-Sol applies a fixed rubric: we retain an
item only when the problem is well posed; the canonical answer is valid,
consistent with the worked solution, and compatible with the
final-answer evaluator; and the worked solution is valid or has only a
minor issue that does not affect correctness. We exclude any item that
cannot be validated under these criteria. Thus, cleaning depends on
problem, answer, and reference quality rather than model performance.
Code problems are instead filtered by execution
(Appendix [B.3](#A2.SS3 "B.3 CodeContests and LiveCodeBench ‣ Appendix B Data and common setup ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")).
Appendices [B.4](#A2.SS4 "B.4 Training ‣ Appendix B Data and common setup ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")-[B.6](#A2.SS6 "B.6 Evaluation ‣ Appendix B Data and common setup ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")
then describe the common training, prompt, and evaluation setup.

### B.1 OpenThoughts

From a 5,440-row candidate pool drawn from the mathematical reasoning
subset of OpenThoughts ([Guha et al., 2026](#bib.bib14)), auditing and
deduplication leave 3,322 unique usable examples. We retain a fixed
3,200-example subset for the
Figure [1](#S1.F1 "Figure 1 ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")
training runs and the primary OpenThoughts analyses.

### B.2 Hendrycks MATH Level 5

Of the 2,304 official Hendrycks MATH Level-5 rows ([Hendrycks et al.,
2021](#bib.bib17)), 2,242 pass the audit and constitute the cleaned
Level-5 panel. For the separate 3,200-example training set, we
deduplicate this panel, retain 2,241 usable Level-5 items, and add 959
audited Level-4 items.

### B.3 CodeContests and LiveCodeBench

For CodeContests ([Li et al., 2022](#bib.bib29)), we retain Python 3
problems with non-generated tests and reference programs that pass
execution, yielding 3,200 training problems and a disjoint 128-problem
held-out set. Exact and near-duplicate checks find no overlap with the
131-problem LiveCodeBench v6 panel ([Jain et al., 2025](#bib.bib30)).

### B.4 Training

Our primary Qwen3 mathematics experiments train
[Qwen3-1.7B](https://huggingface.co/Qwen/Qwen3-1.7B),
[Qwen3-4B](https://huggingface.co/Qwen/Qwen3-4B), and
[Qwen3-8B](https://huggingface.co/Qwen/Qwen3-8B) students ([Yang et al.,
2025](#bib.bib13)). Here and throughout, Base denotes the unchanged
checkpoint before additional training. The teacher is a frozen copy of
that checkpoint, and only student LoRA parameters are updated ([Hu et
al., 2022](#bib.bib15)). We train with veRL ([Sheng et al.,
2025](#bib.bib16)) on the fixed 3,200-example OpenThoughts subset
described in
Appendix [B.1](#A2.SS1 "B.1 OpenThoughts ‣ Appendix B Data and common setup ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models").
Each run takes 100 optimizer steps with a global batch size of 32,
processing every example once. Compared conditions within a scale share
three seeds coupling data order, LoRA initialization, and rollout
randomness. All local training and generation runs use NVIDIA H100 GPUs.

|  Training component |  Setting |
|----|----|
|  Update unit |  One on-policy response per problem; global batch 32; 100 updates (one pass over 3,200 examples). |
|  Student rollout |  Maximum 1,024 response tokens; temperature 1.1; top-p 0.95; top-k 20; presence penalty 0. |
|  Scoring |  The frozen teacher and current student score the same sampled response tokens at every prefix under their respective inputs; loss temperature 1.1. |
|  Objective |  Standard teacher-to-student forward KL; each vocabulary summand is upper-clipped at 0.05 before summation, then averaged over response positions; no post-vocabulary soft cap. |
|  Trainable parameters |  LoRA rank 64, alpha 128; target modules: q_proj, k_proj, v_proj, o_proj, gate_proj, up_proj, and down_proj. |
|  Optimizer |  AdamW; constant learning rate $`5\times 10^{-6}`$; no warmup; weight decay 0; gradient-norm clip 0.1. |
|  Precision and context |  Student/FSDP bfloat16; frozen-teacher scoring float32; maximum sequence length 20,000 tokens (up to 18,976 prompt tokens plus 1,024 response tokens). |

Table B1: Default training setup.

|  Evaluation parameter |  Value |
|----|----|
|  Samples per problem |  12 |
|  Decoding |  Temperature 1.0; top-p 1.0; top-k disabled (-1); min-p 0; presence penalty 0. |
|  Maximum response length |  38,912 tokens |
|  Maximum context length |  40,960 tokens |
|  Inference precision |  bfloat16 |
|  Scoring |  Last well-formed \boxed{...} expression after any closed thinking block; mathematical-equivalence grading. |

Table B2: Five-benchmark reference-free evaluation setup.

Table [B1](#A2.SS4 "B.4 Training ‣ Appendix B Data and common setup ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")
summarizes the default training configuration. We reimplement the OPSD
method of [Zhao et al. (2026a)](#bib.bib5) in veRL using the published
configuration as a starting point. We use the reported learning rate of
$`5\times 10^{-6}`$ and the released default clipping threshold of 0.05
at every scale. The released 8B Thinking-ON script uses 0.06. The
released 4B and 8B Thinking-OFF scripts instead use $`10^{-6}`$ and
$`10^{-7}`$, respectively. We hold the threshold fixed across our
conditions. The released loss function averages over valid tokens in
each call, whereas our implementation normalizes by the valid-token
count across the global minibatch.

### B.5 Thinking modes and prompts

Thinking ON and OFF are implemented with the Qwen3 chat template’s
enable_thinking flag. The mode is set independently for teacher scoring,
student rollouts, and evaluation, and each experiment states the modes
it uses. All other prompt text and sampling parameters are held fixed
within a comparison. The student and teacher use the following inputs.

Student and problem-only-teacher input:

with another problem’s worked solution.

### B.6 Evaluation

The public mathematics suite contains all problems from AIME 2024, AIME
2025, AIME 2026, HMMT November 2025, and HMMT February 2026 ([Zhang and
Math-AI Team, 2024](#bib.bib18); [MathArena, 2025a](#bib.bib19);
[MathArena, 2026a](#bib.bib20); [MathArena, 2025b](#bib.bib21);
[MathArena, 2026b](#bib.bib22)): 30, 30, 30, 30, and 33 items, and the
macro average gives each benchmark equal weight. This suite is evaluated
without the privileged solution under the stated Qwen3 thinking mode.
RQ-specific audits give their own input and scoring protocols.
Table [B2](#A2.SS4 "B.4 Training ‣ Appendix B Data and common setup ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")
summarizes the suite’s configuration. Avg@$`k`$ is mean accuracy over
$`k`$ sampled responses per problem, and pass@$`k`$ is the fraction of
problems with at least one correct response among $`k`$. Intervals over
three training seeds are descriptive paired seed-bootstrap intervals
rather than inferential confidence intervals.

\FloatBarrier

## Appendix C Figure [1](#S1.F1 "Figure 1 ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models"): complete results

Figure [1](#S1.F1 "Figure 1 ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")
uses the common setup in
Appendix [B](#A2 "Appendix B Data and common setup ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models").
Figure [1](#S1.F1 "Figure 1 ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")a
crosses teacher mode {ON, OFF}, student rollout mode {ON, OFF}, and
evaluation mode {ON, OFF} at each model scale.
Figure [1](#S1.F1 "Figure 1 ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")b
fixes the teacher to Thinking ON and the student rollout to Thinking
OFF, and changes only whether the frozen teacher sees the privileged
worked solution or the problem alone.

Checkpoints at updates 20, 40, 60, 80, and 100 are evaluated on the
five-benchmark suite with decoding seed 0.
Figure [1](#S1.F1 "Figure 1 ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")
plots means over three training seeds. The frozen Base is evaluated
independently with decoding seeds 0, 1, and 2 for every model and
evaluation mode. Dashed lines show its three-seed mean and gray bands
its 95% seed-bootstrap interval. Colored intervals for
checkpoint-minus-Base differences use 50,000 independent two-sample
bootstrap resamples of the three training seeds and three Base decoding
seeds. They are descriptive at n = 3 per side. In
Tables [C1a](#A3.SS1 "C.1 Figure a: thinking-mode factorial ‣ Appendix C Figure : complete results ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")–[C2c](#A3.SS2 "C.2 Figure b: privileged-solution versus problem-only teacher ‣ Appendix C Figure : complete results ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models"),
trained-cell accuracies and Base-relative deltas use these corresponding
means, and the final column reports the same two-sample interval for the
macro delta.

privileged-solution arm is the Thinking-ON teacher, Thinking-OFF student
rows of
Tables [C1a](#A3.SS1 "C.1 Figure a: thinking-mode factorial ‣ Appendix C Figure : complete results ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models")–[C1c](#A3.SS1 "C.1 Figure a: thinking-mode factorial ‣ Appendix C Figure : complete results ‣ Privileged Information Does Not Guarantee Privileged Guidance: Auditing On-Policy Self-Distillation for Reasoning Models").
````
